I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.

But lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. And the clubs I used to go to, the ones that kind of gave shape to my week… I’ve pulled away from those too. So yes, it’s affected my days, and I suppose my relationships too, because I’m just not there the way I used to be.

People kept saying time would help, and I know they meant well—my neighbor said the same thing after her husband passed, though that’s a different thing, I know—but it hasn’t really eased.\n\nIt’s like I’m carrying this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger all the time. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your friend's death affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.\n\nBut lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. Yes… I suppose that’s one of the saddest parts. I used to plan my garden beds months ahead—what would go where, which bulbs needed moving. I’d quilt with the ladies, too, and we’d fuss over fabrics like it was some grand important decision. And theater outings, book club… oh, I used to argue at book club, happily, not in a mean way, just lively.

Yes… I do. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense—there’s one street with a bakery I used to like, and now I end up circling around by the pharmacy instead, which is silly because their parking lot is always a mess…

That was about two years and three months ago now, though sometimes I still catch myself counting it by seasons—like, “that was before the lilacs,” or “after the fall trip,” you know.\n\nBut yes… the guilt and fear and anger, they’ve been there since then. About two years and three months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.     "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is… well, I haven’t been myself since my friend was killed. People kept saying time would help, and I know they meant well—my neighbor said the same thing after her husband passed, though that’s a different thing, I know—but it hasn’t really eased.\n\nIt’s like I’m carrying this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger all the time. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. I try to, I really do. I’ll put things on the calendar like I used to—planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, a theater outing, book club. I used to look forward to all of that. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I suppose some days are quieter than others, you know—like when the weather is good and I’m fussing with something small—but it hasn’t really gone away. It’s sort of been threaded through everything since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I try to, I really do. I’ll put things on the calendar like I used to—planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, a theater outing, book club. I used to look forward to all of that. I could spend an entire afternoon deciding where the marigolds should go, or arguing about some novel over coffee, you know, the silly kind of arguing that’s actually fun.\n\nNow I look at those same things written down and… there’s almost nothing there. Like they belong to someone I used to be. Sometimes I still go, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Trauma_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"I go out of my way to avoid that intersection and I have stopped signing up for most tours or safety meetings because I cannot bear the conversations that circle back to what happened there.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I’ve stopped a few things. I don’t sign up for those walking tours anymore, hardly any tours really, and I avoid the safety meetings too—those community ones where people start talking about routes and lighting and what happened at that intersection.

I know it sounds foolish, because sometimes they’re meant to help, but I just can’t sit there while the conversation circles back to that place. And I go out of my way not to pass that intersection now. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense. I tell myself I’m just avoiding traffic, but… no. It’s that.

I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, I still write them down sometimes, almost out of habit—garden beds in spring, quilting with the ladies, the theater schedule, book club. I used to really look forward to those things. I’d spend ages fussing over which flowers should go where, or get so caught up talking about a book that we’d all be laughing over coffee.

Now I look at those same things on the calendar and I feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for someone else, or for the person I used to be. I can’t quite imagine enjoying them the way I did before.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is… well, I haven’t been myself since my friend was killed. People kept saying time would help, and I know they meant well—my neighbor said the same thing after her husband passed, though that’s a different thing, I know—but it hasn’t really eased.\n\nIt’s like I’m carrying this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger all the time. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your friend's death affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.\n\nBut lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. And the clubs I used to go to, the ones that kind of gave shape to my week… I’ve pulled away from those too. So yes, it’s affected my days, and I suppose my relationships too, because I’m just not there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s one of the saddest parts. I used to plan my garden beds months ahead—what would go where, which bulbs needed moving. I’d quilt with the ladies, too, and we’d fuss over fabrics like it was some grand important decision. And theater outings, book club… oh, I used to argue at book club, happily, not in a mean way, just lively.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be, and I’m only looking at her schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance of people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense—there’s one street with a bakery I used to like, and now I end up circling around by the pharmacy instead, which is silly because their parking lot is always a mess…\n\nBut yes, I avoid it. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours and those safety meetings, because someone always starts talking about crosswalks or “what happened there,” and I just can’t bear it. I can feel myself wanting to get up and leave before the conversation even gets there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since it happened… since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now, though sometimes I still catch myself counting it by seasons—like, “that was before the lilacs,” or “after the fall trip,” you know.\n\nBut yes… the guilt and fear and anger, they’ve been there since then. About two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present every day since your friend's death, or have there been periods where you felt somewhat better?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since it happened… since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago. I suppose some days are quieter than others, you know—like when the weather is good and I’m fussing with something small—but it hasn’t really gone away. It’s sort of been threaded through everything since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I try to, I really do. I’ll put things on the calendar like I used to—planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, a theater outing, book club. I used to look forward to all of that. I could spend an entire afternoon deciding where the marigolds should go, or arguing about some novel over coffee, you know, the silly kind of arguing that’s actually fun.\n\nNow I look at those same things written down and… there’s almost nothing there. Like they belong to someone I used to be. Sometimes I still go, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve stopped a few things. I don’t sign up for those walking tours anymore, hardly any tours really, and I avoid the safety meetings too—those community ones where people start talking about routes and lighting and what happened at that intersection.\n\nI know it sounds foolish, because sometimes they’re meant to help, but I just can’t sit there while the conversation circles back to that place. And I go out of my way not to pass that intersection now. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense. I tell myself I’m just avoiding traffic, but… no. It’s that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've not only stopped doing but also feel like you've lost all interest in, to the point where you can't imagine enjoying them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still write them down sometimes, almost out of habit—garden beds in spring, quilting with the ladies, the theater schedule, book club. I used to really look forward to those things. I’d spend ages fussing over which flowers should go where, or get so caught up talking about a book that we’d all be laughing over coffee.\n\nNow I look at those same things on the calendar and I feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for someone else, or for the person I used to be. I can’t quite imagine enjoying them the way I did before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Yes… more than three months. It’s been going on since my friend was killed on that walking tour, which was about two years and three months ago now. Funny, I still remember the season by what was blooming then—I’d just been thinking the hydrangeas needed cutting back—but yes, it’s been that long.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is… well, I haven’t been myself since my friend was killed. People kept saying time would help, and I know they meant well—my neighbor said the same thing after her husband passed, though that’s a different thing, I know—but it hasn’t really eased.\n\nIt’s like I’m carrying this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger all the time. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your friend's death affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.\n\nBut lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. And the clubs I used to go to, the ones that kind of gave shape to my week… I’ve pulled away from those too. So yes, it’s affected my days, and I suppose my relationships too, because I’m just not there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s one of the saddest parts. I used to plan my garden beds months ahead—what would go where, which bulbs needed moving. I’d quilt with the ladies, too, and we’d fuss over fabrics like it was some grand important decision. And theater outings, book club… oh, I used to argue at book club, happily, not in a mean way, just lively.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be, and I’m only looking at her schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance of people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense—there’s one street with a bakery I used to like, and now I end up circling around by the pharmacy instead, which is silly because their parking lot is always a mess…\n\nBut yes, I avoid it. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours and those safety meetings, because someone always starts talking about crosswalks or “what happened there,” and I just can’t bear it. I can feel myself wanting to get up and leave before the conversation even gets there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since it happened… since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now, though sometimes I still catch myself counting it by seasons—like, “that was before the lilacs,” or “after the fall trip,” you know.\n\nBut yes… the guilt and fear and anger, they’ve been there since then. About two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present every day since your friend's death, or have there been periods where you felt somewhat better?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since it happened… since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago. I suppose some days are quieter than others, you know—like when the weather is good and I’m fussing with something small—but it hasn’t really gone away. It’s sort of been threaded through everything since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I try to, I really do. I’ll put things on the calendar like I used to—planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, a theater outing, book club. I used to look forward to all of that. I could spend an entire afternoon deciding where the marigolds should go, or arguing about some novel over coffee, you know, the silly kind of arguing that’s actually fun.\n\nNow I look at those same things written down and… there’s almost nothing there. Like they belong to someone I used to be. Sometimes I still go, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve stopped a few things. I don’t sign up for those walking tours anymore, hardly any tours really, and I avoid the safety meetings too—those community ones where people start talking about routes and lighting and what happened at that intersection.\n\nI know it sounds foolish, because sometimes they’re meant to help, but I just can’t sit there while the conversation circles back to that place. And I go out of my way not to pass that intersection now. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense. I tell myself I’m just avoiding traffic, but… no. It’s that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've not only stopped doing but also feel like you've lost all interest in, to the point where you can't imagine enjoying them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still write them down sometimes, almost out of habit—garden beds in spring, quilting with the ladies, the theater schedule, book club. I used to really look forward to those things. I’d spend ages fussing over which flowers should go where, or get so caught up talking about a book that we’d all be laughing over coffee.\n\nNow I look at those same things on the calendar and I feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for someone else, or for the person I used to be. I can’t quite imagine enjoying them the way I did before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms for more than three months since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. It’s been going on since my friend was killed on that walking tour, which was about two years and three months ago now. Funny, I still remember the season by what was blooming then—I’d just been thinking the hydrangeas needed cutting back—but yes, it’s been that long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Yes… I don’t think there’s been a real break from it. It’s been there since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago.

I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your friend's death affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.\n\nBut lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. And the clubs I used to go to, the ones that kind of gave shape to my week… I’ve pulled away from those too. So yes, it’s affected my days, and I suppose my relationships too, because I’m just not there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s one of the saddest parts. I used to plan my garden beds months ahead—what would go where, which bulbs needed moving. I’d quilt with the ladies, too, and we’d fuss over fabrics like it was some grand important decision. And theater outings, book club… oh, I used to argue at book club, happily, not in a mean way, just lively.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be, and I’m only looking at her schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance of people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense—there’s one street with a bakery I used to like, and now I end up circling around by the pharmacy instead, which is silly because their parking lot is always a mess…\n\nBut yes, I avoid it. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours and those safety meetings, because someone always starts talking about crosswalks or “what happened there,” and I just can’t bear it. I can feel myself wanting to get up and leave before the conversation even gets there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since it happened… since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now, though sometimes I still catch myself counting it by seasons—like, “that was before the lilacs,” or “after the fall trip,” you know.\n\nBut yes… the guilt and fear and anger, they’ve been there since then. About two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present every day since your friend's death, or have there been periods where you felt somewhat better?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since it happened… since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago. I suppose some days are quieter than others, you know—like when the weather is good and I’m fussing with something small—but it hasn’t really gone away. I don’t sign up for those walking tours anymore, hardly any tours really, and I avoid the safety meetings too—those community ones where people start talking about routes and lighting and what happened at that intersection.\n\nI know it sounds foolish, because sometimes they’re meant to help, but I just can’t sit there while the conversation circles back to that place. And I go out of my way not to pass that intersection now. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense. I tell myself I’m just avoiding traffic, but… no. Not really.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you've lost interest in things that were once important to you, to the point where you can't imagine finding joy in them again?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 69 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Loss_of_Interest\nmanifestation: \"I used to plan my garden beds, quilt with the ladies, go to theater outings, and argue happily at book club, but now I look at those things on the calendar and feel almost nothing, like they belong to someone I used to be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I do. I know I’ve said bits of this already, but it’s the clearest way I can explain it. I used to sit with my little notebook and plan the garden beds, which tulips would come up first, whether the zinnias would clash with the salvia—silly things, but they mattered to me.

And quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club… I used to love all that. We’d argue about endings and characters like they were real people, and then someone would bring lemon bars and we’d forget what we were arguing about.

Now I see those things written on the calendar and I just… don’t feel much of anything. It’s like they belong to a woman I used to know, not me. I can’t really picture feeling happy doing them again, not the way I did before.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is… well, I haven’t been myself since my friend was killed. People kept saying time would help, and I know they meant well—my neighbor said the same thing after her husband passed, though that’s a different thing, I know—but it hasn’t really eased.\n\nIt’s like I’m carrying this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger all the time. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your friend's death affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.\n\nBut lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. And the clubs I used to go to, the ones that kind of gave shape to my week… I’ve pulled away from those too. So yes, it’s affected my days, and I suppose my relationships too, because I’m just not there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s one of the saddest parts. I used to plan my garden beds months ahead—what would go where, which bulbs needed moving. I’d quilt with the ladies, too, and we’d fuss over fabrics like it was some grand important decision. And theater outings, book club… oh, I used to argue at book club, happily, not in a mean way, just lively.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be, and I’m only looking at her schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance of people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense—there’s one street with a bakery I used to like, and now I end up circling around by the pharmacy instead, which is silly because their parking lot is always a mess…\n\nBut yes, I avoid it. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours and those safety meetings, because someone always starts talking about crosswalks or “what happened there,” and I just can’t bear it. I can feel myself wanting to get up and leave before the conversation even gets there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since it happened… since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now, though sometimes I still catch myself counting it by seasons—like, “that was before the lilacs,” or “after the fall trip,” you know.\n\nBut yes… the guilt and fear and anger, they’ve been there since then. About two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present every day since your friend's death, or have there been periods where you felt somewhat better?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since it happened… since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago. I suppose some days are quieter than others, you know—like when the weather is good and I’m fussing with something small—but it hasn’t really gone away. It’s sort of been threaded through everything since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I try to, I really do. I’ll put things on the calendar like I used to—planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, a theater outing, book club. I used to look forward to all of that. I could spend an entire afternoon deciding where the marigolds should go, or arguing about some novel over coffee, you know, the silly kind of arguing that’s actually fun.\n\nNow I look at those same things written down and… there’s almost nothing there. Like they belong to someone I used to be. Sometimes I still go, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve stopped a few things. I don’t sign up for those walking tours anymore, hardly any tours really, and I avoid the safety meetings too—those community ones where people start talking about routes and lighting and what happened at that intersection.\n\nI know it sounds foolish, because sometimes they’re meant to help, but I just can’t sit there while the conversation circles back to that place. And I go out of my way not to pass that intersection now. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense. I tell myself I’m just avoiding traffic, but… no. It’s that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've not only stopped doing but also feel like you've lost all interest in, to the point where you can't imagine enjoying them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still write them down sometimes, almost out of habit—garden beds in spring, quilting with the ladies, the theater schedule, book club. I used to really look forward to those things. I’d spend ages fussing over which flowers should go where, or get so caught up talking about a book that we’d all be laughing over coffee.\n\nNow I look at those same things on the calendar and I feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for someone else, or for the person I used to be. I can’t quite imagine enjoying them the way I did before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms for more than three months since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. It’s been going on since my friend was killed on that walking tour, which was about two years and three months ago now. Funny, I still remember the season by what was blooming then—I’d just been thinking the hydrangeas needed cutting back—but yes, it’s been that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these symptoms without any significant breaks or periods where they completely went away since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I don’t think there’s been a real break from it. It’s been there since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago.\n\nSome days are quieter, I suppose—like when the weather is nice and I get distracted by the garden or a neighbor stops by to ask about something silly, the recycling bins or her roses—but it hasn’t completely gone away. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is… well, I haven’t been myself since my friend was killed. People kept saying time would help, and I know they meant well—my neighbor said the same thing after her husband passed, though that’s a different thing, I know—but it hasn’t really eased.\n\nIt’s like I’m carrying this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger all the time. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your friend's death affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.\n\nBut lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. And the clubs I used to go to, the ones that kind of gave shape to my week… I’ve pulled away from those too. So yes, it’s affected my days, and I suppose my relationships too, because I’m just not there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s one of the saddest parts. I used to plan my garden beds months ahead—what would go where, which bulbs needed moving. I’d quilt with the ladies, too, and we’d fuss over fabrics like it was some grand important decision. And theater outings, book club… oh, I used to argue at book club, happily, not in a mean way, just lively.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be, and I’m only looking at her schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance of people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense—there’s one street with a bakery I used to like, and now I end up circling around by the pharmacy instead, which is silly because their parking lot is always a mess…\n\nBut yes, I avoid it. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours and those safety meetings, because someone always starts talking about crosswalks or “what happened there,” and I just can’t bear it. I can feel myself wanting to get up and leave before the conversation even gets there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since it happened… since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now, though sometimes I still catch myself counting it by seasons—like, “that was before the lilacs,” or “after the fall trip,” you know.\n\nBut yes… the guilt and fear and anger, they’ve been there since then. About two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present every day since your friend's death, or have there been periods where you felt somewhat better?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since it happened… since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago. I suppose some days are quieter than others, you know—like when the weather is good and I’m fussing with something small—but it hasn’t really gone away. It’s sort of been threaded through everything since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I try to, I really do. Like they belong to someone I used to be. Sometimes I still go, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve stopped a few things. I don’t sign up for those walking tours anymore, hardly any tours really, and I avoid the safety meetings too—those community ones where people start talking about routes and lighting and what happened at that intersection.\n\nI know it sounds foolish, because sometimes they’re meant to help, but I just can’t sit there while the conversation circles back to that place. And I go out of my way not to pass that intersection now. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense. I tell myself I’m just avoiding traffic, but… no. It’s that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've not only stopped doing but also feel like you've lost all interest in, to the point where you can't imagine enjoying them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S074\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these symptoms without any significant breaks or periods where they completely went away since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I don’t think there’s been a real break from it. It’s been there since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago.\n\nSome days are quieter, I suppose—like when the weather is nice and I get distracted by the garden or a neighbor stops by to ask about something silly, the recycling bins or her roses—but it hasn’t completely gone away. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that were once important to you, to the point where you can't imagine finding joy in them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I know I’ve said bits of this already, but it’s the clearest way I can explain it. I used to sit with my little notebook and plan the garden beds, which tulips would come up first, whether the zinnias would clash with the salvia—silly things, but they mattered to me.\n\nAnd quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club… I used to love all that. We’d argue about endings and characters like they were real people, and then someone would bring lemon bars and we’d forget what we were arguing about.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… don’t feel much of anything. It’s like they belong to a woman I used to know, not me. I can’t really picture feeling happy doing them again, not the way I did before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of them, I think. The garden especially—I used to spend ages planning the beds, where the tulips would go, whether the colors would look right. And quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club… we used to get so worked up over the books, like it mattered terribly.\n\nNow I see those things on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It’s strange, like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been experiencing these symptoms for at least six months since your friend's death?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Yes… yes, it’s been much longer than six months. It started after my friend was killed on that walking tour, and that was about two years and three months ago now.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is… well, I haven’t been myself since my friend was killed. People kept saying time would help, and I know they meant well—my neighbor said the same thing after her husband passed, though that’s a different thing, I know—but it hasn’t really eased.\n\nIt’s like I’m carrying this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger all the time. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your friend's death affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.\n\nBut lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. And the clubs I used to go to, the ones that kind of gave shape to my week… I’ve pulled away from those too. So yes, it’s affected my days, and I suppose my relationships too, because I’m just not there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s one of the saddest parts. I used to plan my garden beds months ahead—what would go where, which bulbs needed moving. I’d quilt with the ladies, too, and we’d fuss over fabrics like it was some grand important decision. And theater outings, book club… oh, I used to argue at book club, happily, not in a mean way, just lively.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be, and I’m only looking at her schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance of people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense—there’s one street with a bakery I used to like, and now I end up circling around by the pharmacy instead, which is silly because their parking lot is always a mess…\n\nBut yes, I avoid it. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours and those safety meetings, because someone always starts talking about crosswalks or “what happened there,” and I just can’t bear it. I can feel myself wanting to get up and leave before the conversation even gets there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since it happened… since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now, though sometimes I still catch myself counting it by seasons—like, “that was before the lilacs,” or “after the fall trip,” you know.\n\nBut yes… the guilt and fear and anger, they’ve been there since then. About two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present every day since your friend's death, or have there been periods where you felt somewhat better?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since it happened… since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago. I suppose some days are quieter than others, you know—like when the weather is good and I’m fussing with something small—but it hasn’t really gone away. It’s sort of been threaded through everything since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I try to, I really do. I’ll put things on the calendar like I used to—planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, a theater outing, book club. I used to look forward to all of that. I could spend an entire afternoon deciding where the marigolds should go, or arguing about some novel over coffee, you know, the silly kind of arguing that’s actually fun.\n\nNow I look at those same things written down and… there’s almost nothing there. Like they belong to someone I used to be. Sometimes I still go, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve stopped a few things. I don’t sign up for those walking tours anymore, hardly any tours really, and I avoid the safety meetings too—those community ones where people start talking about routes and lighting and what happened at that intersection.\n\nI know it sounds foolish, because sometimes they’re meant to help, but I just can’t sit there while the conversation circles back to that place. And I go out of my way not to pass that intersection now. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense. I tell myself I’m just avoiding traffic, but… no. It’s that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've not only stopped doing but also feel like you've lost all interest in, to the point where you can't imagine enjoying them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still write them down sometimes, almost out of habit—garden beds in spring, quilting with the ladies, the theater schedule, book club. I used to really look forward to those things. I’d spend ages fussing over which flowers should go where, or get so caught up talking about a book that we’d all be laughing over coffee.\n\nNow I look at those same things on the calendar and I feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for someone else, or for the person I used to be. I can’t quite imagine enjoying them the way I did before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms for more than three months since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. It’s been going on since my friend was killed on that walking tour, which was about two years and three months ago now. Funny, I still remember the season by what was blooming then—I’d just been thinking the hydrangeas needed cutting back—but yes, it’s been that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these symptoms without any significant breaks or periods where they completely went away since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I don’t think there’s been a real break from it. It’s been there since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago.\n\nSome days are quieter, I suppose—like when the weather is nice and I get distracted by the garden or a neighbor stops by to ask about something silly, the recycling bins or her roses—but it hasn’t completely gone away. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that were once important to you, to the point where you can't imagine finding joy in them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I know I’ve said bits of this already, but it’s the clearest way I can explain it. I used to sit with my little notebook and plan the garden beds, which tulips would come up first, whether the zinnias would clash with the salvia—silly things, but they mattered to me.\n\nAnd quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club… I used to love all that. We’d argue about endings and characters like they were real people, and then someone would bring lemon bars and we’d forget what we were arguing about.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… don’t feel much of anything. I can’t really picture feeling happy doing them again, not the way I did before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of them, I think. Yes… almost all of them, I suppose. I used to fill whole pages planning my garden beds—where the tulips would go, what colors would come up when. And I quilted with the ladies, went to the theater, book club… oh, we used to fuss over those books like we’d written them ourselves.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is… well, I haven’t been myself since my friend was killed. People kept saying time would help, and I know they meant well—my neighbor said the same thing after her husband passed, though that’s a different thing, I know—but it hasn’t really eased.\n\nIt’s like I’m carrying this constant mix of guilt and fear and anger all the time. I get tired of feeling that way, and I don’t seem to be able to just move past it the way everyone seemed to think I would.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about your friend's death in a way that feels intrusive or hard to control?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. It comes in before I can even brace myself. Like if I’m at a crosswalk and the signal changes, or I hear a truck sort of growling past—oh, those big delivery trucks, they’re everywhere now, even on the smaller streets near my building—or even if I see one of those walking-tour maps with the little routes marked out, suddenly the whole thing is right there again.\n\nI can see her stepping off the curb, clear as anything, and I’m back in it before I can stop it. It’s not like I sit down and decide to think about it. It just… forces its way in.\n\nDoctor: Are these thoughts and feelings about your friend's death affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I like to admit, really. I used to help organize programs at the senior center—you know, the talks, little outings, sometimes arranging who brought what for the luncheons—and I handled some of the volunteer budget things too. I was always good with numbers, actually; my father used to say I balanced a checkbook better than the bank did.\n\nBut lately I just can’t keep up reliably. I miss things, or I feel too overwhelmed to be the person people are counting on, so I’ve had to step back from a lot of that. And the clubs I used to go to, the ones that kind of gave shape to my week… I’ve pulled away from those too. So yes, it’s affected my days, and I suppose my relationships too, because I’m just not there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy but no longer find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose that’s one of the saddest parts. I used to plan my garden beds months ahead—what would go where, which bulbs needed moving. I’d quilt with the ladies, too, and we’d fuss over fabrics like it was some grand important decision. And theater outings, book club… oh, I used to argue at book club, happily, not in a mean way, just lively.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… feel almost nothing. Like they belong to someone I used to be, and I’m only looking at her schedule.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance of people, places, or activities that remind you of your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I go out of my way not to pass that intersection. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense—there’s one street with a bakery I used to like, and now I end up circling around by the pharmacy instead, which is silly because their parking lot is always a mess…\n\nBut yes, I avoid it. And I’ve stopped signing up for most of the walking tours and those safety meetings, because someone always starts talking about crosswalks or “what happened there,” and I just can’t bear it. I can feel myself wanting to get up and leave before the conversation even gets there.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of guilt, fear, and anger since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Since it happened… since she was killed on that walking tour. That was about two years and three months ago now, though sometimes I still catch myself counting it by seasons—like, “that was before the lilacs,” or “after the fall trip,” you know.\n\nBut yes… the guilt and fear and anger, they’ve been there since then. About two years and three months.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been present every day since your friend's death, or have there been periods where you felt somewhat better?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since it happened… since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago. I suppose some days are quieter than others, you know—like when the weather is good and I’m fussing with something small—but it hasn’t really gone away. It’s sort of been threaded through everything since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find yourself looking forward to or enjoying, even if it's just a little?\n\nPatient: I try to, I really do. I’ll put things on the calendar like I used to—planning the garden beds, quilting with the ladies, a theater outing, book club. I used to look forward to all of that. I could spend an entire afternoon deciding where the marigolds should go, or arguing about some novel over coffee, you know, the silly kind of arguing that’s actually fun.\n\nNow I look at those same things written down and… there’s almost nothing there. Like they belong to someone I used to be. Sometimes I still go, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than enjoying it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’ve stopped a few things. I don’t sign up for those walking tours anymore, hardly any tours really, and I avoid the safety meetings too—those community ones where people start talking about routes and lighting and what happened at that intersection.\n\nI know it sounds foolish, because sometimes they’re meant to help, but I just can’t sit there while the conversation circles back to that place. And I go out of my way not to pass that intersection now. I’ll take a longer route, even if it makes no sense. I tell myself I’m just avoiding traffic, but… no. It’s that.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've not only stopped doing but also feel like you've lost all interest in, to the point where you can't imagine enjoying them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still write them down sometimes, almost out of habit—garden beds in spring, quilting with the ladies, the theater schedule, book club. I used to really look forward to those things. I’d spend ages fussing over which flowers should go where, or get so caught up talking about a book that we’d all be laughing over coffee.\n\nNow I look at those same things on the calendar and I feel… almost nothing. Like they’re for someone else, or for the person I used to be. I can’t quite imagine enjoying them the way I did before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these symptoms for more than three months since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… more than three months. It’s been going on since my friend was killed on that walking tour, which was about two years and three months ago now. Funny, I still remember the season by what was blooming then—I’d just been thinking the hydrangeas needed cutting back—but yes, it’s been that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these symptoms without any significant breaks or periods where they completely went away since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I don’t think there’s been a real break from it. It’s been there since my friend was killed on that walking tour, about two years and three months ago.\n\nSome days are quieter, I suppose—like when the weather is nice and I get distracted by the garden or a neighbor stops by to ask about something silly, the recycling bins or her roses—but it hasn’t completely gone away. Not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things that were once important to you, to the point where you can't imagine finding joy in them again?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I know I’ve said bits of this already, but it’s the clearest way I can explain it. I used to sit with my little notebook and plan the garden beds, which tulips would come up first, whether the zinnias would clash with the salvia—silly things, but they mattered to me.\n\nAnd quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club… I used to love all that. We’d argue about endings and characters like they were real people, and then someone would bring lemon bars and we’d forget what we were arguing about.\n\nNow I see those things written on the calendar and I just… don’t feel much of anything. It’s like they belong to a woman I used to know, not me. I can’t really picture feeling happy doing them again, not the way I did before.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in most things that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… most of them, I think. The garden especially—I used to spend ages planning the beds, where the tulips would go, whether the colors would look right. And quilting with the ladies, theater outings, book club… we used to get so worked up over the books, like it mattered terribly.\n\nNow I see those things on the calendar and I feel almost nothing. It’s strange, like they belong to someone I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing these symptoms for at least six months since your friend's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it’s been much longer than six months. It started after my friend was killed on that walking tour, and that was about two years and three months ago now.\n\nI remember because the seasons have gone around more than twice since then—the bulbs, the leaves, all of it—and it’s still been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in almost all activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost all of them, I suppose. I used to fill whole pages planning my garden beds—where the tulips would go, what colors would come up when. And I quilted with the ladies, went to the theater, book club… oh, we used to fuss over those books like we’d written them ourselves.\n\nNow I look at those things on the calendar and it’s just… blank. Like they’re appointments for someone else. Someone I used to be, maybe. I don’t feel that pull toward them anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yes… most days, really. It’s like this knot of guilt and fear and anger has been with me since she was killed. I kept thinking it would soften, you know, like people said time would do that.
